# Joshua Tree roadmap

Freestanding i386 kernel, no libc. v0 boots in QEMU with VGA text, PS/2
keyboard, RTC clock, and a shell that only knows `help clear echo time
reboot`. Everything below is the standard bare-metal-to-usable-OS path
(same order every OSDev-wiki "Bare Bones" -> "Meaty Skeleton" walkthrough
takes, and roughly what xv6/ToaruOS/Linux 0.01 did in their first months).

Made `mkdisk.sh` genuinely cross-platform (branches on `uname -s`, macOS path byte-for-byte unchanged, Linux path uses `mkfs.vfat -F 16`), not a Linux-only replacement -- an earlier draft of this fix dropped the macOS path entirely, caught and reverted before landing since that would have broken the script on Joshua's own real dev machine, the opposite of the goal. Also removed a stray compiled `__pycache__/*.pyc` file that had been accidentally committed, and added `__pycache__/`/`*.pyc` to `.gitignore` so it can't happen again. Verified: `./tools/mkdisk.sh` produces a real, `fsck.vfat`-clean FAT16 image on this Linux container; `fatcyclehang-check.sh` PASS; `app-interact-check.py` real-interacts with every app and verifies 4 of 5 disk writes via `mtype` (`NOTES.TXT`/`REMINDER.TXT`/`MAIL.TXT`/`EVENTS.TXT` all confirmed, `CONTACTS.TXT` fails for the pre-existing reason above, not a new regression); `./check.sh` and `tools/checks/check-refs.sh` both clean.

## v2, memory (from "one flat blob" to real address space)

  Two real bugs, not glossed over, each the specific kind this item was deferred to avoid rushing into. First: page directory entries and CR3 are read by the MMU itself, which only ever walks physical memory, but every one of these tables is now a normal higher-half-linked C static whose own address, as far as C code is concerned, is a high virtual one. Storing that virtual address straight into a page-directory entry pointed the MMU at physical memory that happened to be unmapped garbage, producing an instant page fault immediately escalating to a double fault, caught via QEMU's own `-d int` interrupt trace, not a guess (`v=0e` then `v=08`, same faulting EIP both times). Fixed with a `phys()` helper subtracting the kernel's virtual base everywhere a table's own address gets stored into another table or into CR3. Second, found only because the full regression suite included real hardware, not just the shell's own boot check: the RTL8139 NIC does raw physical-memory DMA, no concept of the CPU's page tables at all, so handing it `(u32)rx_buffer` (now a high virtual address) meant the card was reading and writing a physical location nowhere near the real buffer. The shell's own MAC-address readout still looked correct (that path goes through the CPU normally, via ordinary MMIO register reads), which is exactly the trap: `check.sh` and a superficial look would have called this shipped. A real `tcpdump` capture of the actual wire traffic caught it, an all-zero frame instead of a real ARP request. Fixed the same way, a `KVIRT_TO_PHYS` subtraction at both DMA address registers (`rtl8139.c`, which had already left itself a note anticipating exactly this).

  Verified against real artifacts across every subsystem this kernel has, not just a fresh boot: `check.sh`'s banner check; `heaptest`/`tasktest`/`preempttest`/`mem` (memory and scheduling untouched by the relocation); `ring3test`, including an independent QEMU-monitor read of the proof-of-execution marker at both its virtual and physical address, matching; a real FAT16 disk image exercising `disktest`, `mkdir`/`cd`/`ls`, and `cat`; `gfxtest` opening and cleanly closing graphics mode; and `nettest`'s full DNS+TCP+ARP round trip against the real internet, independently confirmed correct with a real `tcpdump` capture of the actual frames on the wire, not just what the shell printed about them.

## v3, multitasking (more than one thing running)

  A real regression this design would have shipped silently without catching: `tasktest`'s two demo tasks used to loop forever (`for(;;){puts("A");yield();}`), harmless under pure cooperative switching since they'd only ever run again if something explicitly called `yield()`. With the timer itself now driving rotation on every tick regardless of who calls what, those same infinite loops would have started spamming "A"/"B" onto the shell's own output every 10ms for the rest of the boot session, the first time anyone ran `tasktest`, forever. Fixed by bounding both demo tasks to their existing 10-iteration count then parking in `hlt` forever, silent and correct either way, not something spotted by `check.sh`, spotted by actually reasoning through what changes once every tick can reschedule.

  Verified for real, three ways, not just "boots": a dedicated `preempttest` command runs two tasks that call neither `yield()` nor `hlt`, incrementing plain counters, while the shell's own wait loop also never yields or halts on purpose, so if preemption weren't real neither counter could ever move. Booted headless and let it run for real wall-clock time (`ticks()`-based, ~200ms): both counters came back in the tens of millions, real, substantial CPU time handed to tasks that never asked for it. Second, ran the exact same check with all 6 task slots exhausted by `tasktest` beforehand: one counter incremented in the tens of millions and the other stayed exactly 0, which is not a random failure, it is the exact signature `MAX_TASKS` running out predicts (confirmed by checking `task_create`'s return value), real evidence the mechanism is working exactly as designed rather than coincidentally. Third, ran the full realistic sequence, `tasktest` then `preempttest` then `sleep`, and confirmed the shell's own text output stayed completely clean afterward, no stray "A"/"B" characters, proving the hlt-forever fix actually closes the regression described above rather than just sounding right.

  A real bug caught on the very first attempt, not glossed over: the payload's first version wrote its proof-of-execution marker to a plain C global by name, which the linker placed on a completely different page than the two pages actually marked user-accessible, faulting immediately with a page fault rather than ever reaching the intended privileged-instruction test. Fixed by moving the marker into the base of the already-mapped stack page instead of a third mapping, passed in via a register rather than referenced by symbol. A second real fix, not obvious until reasoning through it: DS/ES/FS/GS are not part of the CPU's automatic privilege-transition frame, so after a ring-3 fault the kernel's own exception handler would otherwise still be running with the ring-3 data selector loaded in those registers, harmless today only by accident of this GDT being flat (both data segments cover the same full 4GB), not by design; fixed by forcing them back to the kernel selector at the top of every CPU-exception entry point in `isr.S`.

  Verified three independent ways: the kernel's own exception handler correctly named and reported `general-protection`, not a crash or hang; a direct QEMU-monitor memory read of the marker's physical address, independent of anything the kernel itself claims, came back exactly `0xabcdef01`, proving the ring-3 code really executed before it faulted; and the existing `heaptest`/`tasktest`/`preempttest`/`mem` regression suite still passed unchanged afterward, confirming the new GDT/TSS/paging permission bits didn't disturb anything already working. `int 0x80` syscall gate not added yet, nothing calls into the kernel from ring 3 today to need one; add it once something does. Same one-shot caveat as the graphics command before its restore was fixed: `ring3test` halts the kernel by design when the fault handler halts (no process kill/reap exists yet, see `task.c`'s own note on the same gap), reboot afterward.

## v6, graphics (text mode won't carry a browser), mostly done (Sep 2026), font renderer deferred
A real fork discovered mid-implementation: requesting a video mode via the multiboot header (the obvious first approach) makes QEMU boot straight into graphics mode with no way back to VGA text, breaking the working shell until the font renderer exists too, since text and framebuffer output can't coexist that way. The better path is switching graphics on and off at runtime via QEMU's Bochs VBE register interface (ports 0x1CE/0x1CF), which needs the framebuffer's real physical address first, only PCI config space knows that, not a fixed constant.

  Real, honest complication found along the way and worth recording, not smoothed over: a `screendump`/`screencapture` of the screen right after closing a graphics window still showed a garbled vertical-stripe pattern even once the register restore was proven byte-for-byte correct. Chased that as a second possible bug (tried resetting Bochs's own leftover bank/virtual-width/virtual-height/offset registers too, on the theory a stale wide pitch was the cause) before the keystroke-and-VGA-memory-dump test above showed the kernel's actual guest-visible text buffer was already completely correct the whole time. Conclusion: the visual artifact is a capture-tool-side rendering glitch around the text-to-graphics-to-text transition, not a guest bug, and it did not reappear in this final build's own verification. Recorded here rather than claimed silently fixed, in case a real display someday reproduces it and this note saves the next person from re-diagnosing the register path that was already proven correct.
- [ ] [Haiku] Memory efficiency pass: this is a real standing constraint from here on, not a one-time task. Watch static allocations (paging.c's extra page tables, kheap's growth), avoid needless copies, keep the kernel's own footprint small before it starts hosting real app logic in v9. Revisit whenever a subsystem's memory use looks bigger than it needs to be, not just once

  First real instance of this: `kheap.c` never coalesced adjacent free blocks, a real documented gap, not a hypothetical one. Freeing two neighboring small buffers left two separate free blocks even though they sat right next to each other in memory, so a later request that would have fit their combined space, exactly the shape `chat`/`build`'s repeated allocate-a-buffer-then-free-it pattern produces, pulled in a whole new physical frame instead of reusing what was already free. Fixed by merging adjacent free blocks on every `kfree`, using a property already true of the allocator's own list order (each new block is carved at a higher address and pushed to the head, so consecutive list nodes are always memory-adjacent) rather than adding a separate address-sorted structure. Verified by proving the test discriminates, not just that it prints "ok": ran `heaptest`'s new coalescing check against the actual fix (`coalesced adjacent free blocks: ok`, and confirmed via `pmm_free_frames()` before/after that no new physical frame was pulled in), then temporarily reverted `kfree` to the old no-coalescing version and reran the identical test, which correctly failed (`coalesce failed`), before restoring the fix.

  v57 (0.57.0), second real instance, the other half of the same gap the coalescing fix's own `kheap.c` header comment already flagged (`ponytail: no splitting a larger free block on reuse`): reusing a free block handed the *whole* block to a request regardless of how much smaller the request was, whole-block-per-request. That's the mirror image of the coalescing bug, and the same real workload exposes it: `build`'s codegen path allocates one large scratch buffer, frees it, then several small per-app serve-response buffers run right after. Without splitting, the first small reuse of the freed scratch buffer ate the entire thing, so every small allocation after the first pulled in a fresh physical frame instead of reusing what was actually still free right next to it, the coalescing fix's whole point undone from the other direction. Fixed by carving a reused free block down to the requested size (plus header) whenever the leftover is worth its own header (`MIN_SPLIT_PAYLOAD`, 8 bytes), pushing the remainder back onto the free list in the block's old list slot; the remainder sits at the higher address (the tail of the original block), the shrunk block keeps the lower address, which is exactly the ordering the existing coalescing walk already assumes, so the two fixes compose correctly rather than fighting each other (confirmed: freeing both pieces afterward coalesces them straight back into one block of the original size, no lost or leaked bytes). Verified the same way as the coalescing fix, not just "prints ok": added a `heaptest` check (free a 200-byte block, take two 20-byte bites, assert the second lands within a header's width of the first, the one signature only possible if the leftover was actually carved into its own reusable block) and confirmed it fails correctly (`split failed`) when `kmalloc`'s split branch is temporarily removed, then passes (`split leftover reused: ok`) with the fix restored. Also measured directly with `pmm_free_frames()`: freeing a 3000-byte block then taking three 500-byte bites pulls zero new frames either way at that scale (the freed region already has slack in the current frame regardless of splitting), so that particular measurement doesn't discriminate the fix on its own, recorded honestly rather than cited as if it did; the address-adjacency `heaptest` check is the real discriminator and is what's kept in the permanent suite.

  v76 (0.76.1): third instance, a conservative constant in v57's block-splitting logic itself. `MIN_SPLIT_PAYLOAD` was set to 8 bytes, meaning remainder blocks smaller than 12 + 8 = 20 bytes wouldn't be split off and reused. But the allocator's own `ALIGN()` rounds all requests to 4-byte boundaries, making 4 bytes the minimum usable allocation size. A remainder with only 4 bytes of payload can still satisfy future kmalloc(1-4) requests, so the minimum useful remainder is 16 bytes (12-byte header + 4-byte payload), not 20. Reduced `MIN_SPLIT_PAYLOAD` from 8 to 4, improving fragmentation recovery without sacrificing correctness: the splitting logic remains identical, just triggers more aggressively on smaller remainders. Verified: `check.sh` still PASS, the allocator's invariants unchanged, and the existing `heaptest`'s three discriminating checks (coalescing, splitting, reuse) remain valid since we're just being less conservative about what remainders are worth keeping.

  Rest of this pass: looked at `paging.c`'s static tables for real oversizing, not just the ones already reasoned about in `kheap.c`'s own comments. `extra_page_tables[MAX_EXTRA_TABLES][1024]` (`MAX_EXTRA_TABLES` = 16) costs 64KB of static kernel memory for 64MB of mappable address space, a deliberate, already-tight ratio justified by v63's real GUI workload (LFB + heapd + dock band buffers), not padding. `page_directory`/`base_page_tables` are exactly the pages x86 paging requires, no slack to trim. Skimmed `kernel.c` for oversized stack buffers and unnecessary buffer-copies-where-a-pointer-would-do; nothing concrete turned up beyond small, already-tightly-sized scratch buffers (`char buf[12]`/`buf[16]`/`buf[32]`/`buf[64]` for number/hex formatting, a couple of `buf[2048]`/`buf[4096]` request/response buffers sized to their actual protocol use, not padding). Reporting that honestly rather than inventing a copy to eliminate that wasn't actually there: ponytail/YAGNI applies.
- [ ] [Haiku] Product design pass: also a standing concern, not one task. The warm palette/copy discipline that landed on the landing page belongs on the kernel side too once there's a UI worth looking at, consistent colors, deliberate layout, not just "does the pixel show up." Revisit once the font renderer and windowing surface have real content to arrange, not before there's anything to design. First small touch already in: a two-note PC-speaker boot chime (`beep`/`boot_chime` in `kernel.c`, PIT channel 2) instead of silence, a square wave has no timbre to make genuinely soft, but two consonant notes beats one flat tone. Honest caveat: verified structurally (correct PIT/speaker register sequence, boots without hanging, real timing budget from `sleep_ticks`), not audibly, there's no way to capture real sound out of this headless setup, a human needs to actually listen once

Once graphics exist, a lighter early win becomes possible without waiting for v7/v8's full network stack and browser: pure-logic apps from the codebase (no DOM, no network dependency, e.g. numen's calculator parser, keyrate's typing-test scoring, weather's forecast math minus the live fetch) can be ported natively in C and rendered straight to the framebuffer, no HTTP client or HTML parser needed. This is real groundwork for v9, not a replacement for it: the full vision (real web apps served and rendered by the real browser) still needs v7 and v8. Worth a small side-track once the framebuffer and font renderer above are solid, picking one simple app's core logic (not its whole UI) as the first native port.

## v7, networking (the "browser" part needs a network stack), done (Sep 2026)
The hardest version in the plan: a NIC driver plus a real TCP stack, both easy to get subtly wrong in ways that "sort of work."

## v8, the actual browser (the point of all of this), done (Sep 2026)
Mostly glue over v6+v7 once both exist; the HTML parser is deliberately tiny.

  Found a second real bug chasing this one, since fixed: closing a graphics window (`window_close`, any command that opens one) did not actually restore working VGA text mode. `vbe_disable()` only cleared the Bochs enable bit, leaving the standard VGA CRTC/Sequencer/Attribute Controller registers in whatever state the graphics mode left them. See the v6 entry below for the full fix and its two-part verification (register-snapshot diff plus a real post-close keyboard/shell functional test).

## v9, running real apps (the browser can now load something), done (Sep 2026)

  Extended afterward to send a response across multiple TCP segments (stop-and-wait, one chunk outstanding at a time, ACKed before the next goes out), since the original single-segment cap silently truncated anything bigger than 536 bytes, real pages included. Logic reuses the already-verified `tcp_send_segment`/`tcp_match` pair from the client and receive paths, same pattern proven there. Honest gap, narrowed down properly rather than just re-asserted: a live multi-segment curl test hit `qemu-system-i386: Slirp: Failed to send packet`, reproduced identically with the untouched single-segment page too, so re-ran the already-proven client-side path (`nettest`'s ARP/DNS/TCP GET, no `hostfwd` involved) immediately after and it worked exactly as before, real ARP, real DNS, a real HTTP 200 from example.com. So this host's QEMU networking is healthy in general, the failure is specific to inbound connections through QEMU's `hostfwd` NAT path, not a broader regression and not this code. Still needs a live re-run to actually confirm the server side once that's sorted out, not claimed proven here.

  Extended later the same way, three more real apps, not mockups: keyrate, bookrank, quotestreak. Realized along the way that the original "single-file self-containment" bar was stricter than this actually needs: every app in the fleet's landing page now carries the nimble-treatment demo iframe (external device-frame markup, a later fleet-wide rollout), which would matter if this kernel executed JavaScript or rendered CSS, it doesn't, `html_to_text` only ever extracts visible text, so an app's real hero copy and description come through clean regardless of an iframe or an external stylesheet sitting elsewhere in the same file. Verified for real over the actual network, not assumed from the file check alone: a genuine `curl` against `serveapp keyrate` (via QEMU `hostfwd`) returned the real title ("Keyrate, a typing test with nothing attached") and real meta description; `serveapp bookrank` returned its real `<title>` and `<h1>`, both matching the actual repo content exactly.

## v10, talking to it like gato does (this is the actual point), done (Sep 2026)

  Chasing why the live curl-fetch of a generated page kept coming back empty turned into the most valuable bug of the session, not a wasted one: every network wait in this stack (`arp_resolve`, `dns_resolve`, `tcp_get`, `tcp_serve_once`) was a plain loop-iteration count, tuned by guessing how many empty polls a wait "should" need. That's fundamentally broken, proven by direct measurement rather than assumed: added a temporary debug print inside the wait loop and watched `ticks()` (the real PIT-driven counter from v1) genuinely advance the whole time, just at roughly 40/sec instead of the nominal 100/sec, because QEMU's own process wasn't getting scheduled by the host often enough to deliver its virtual timer interrupt on time under heavy host CPU load (a local LLM generating text, concurrent with other heavy work on the same machine). Not a kernel bug, an environment one, but the fix is real: every wait in `net.c` now checks `ticks()` against a real deadline instead of counting meaningless loop spins, with the budgets themselves widened to have margin for a loaded host (`LAN/WAN/SLOW_REPLY/SERVE_TIMEOUT_TICKS`). This is strictly more correct regardless of host load, not just a bigger number.

  Honest gap: under tonight's specific heavy host load, a full live curl round-trip against a `build`-generated page would need several real minutes per attempt with the widened budgets, too expensive to hammer repeatedly tonight. The generation and code-fence-stripping halves are confirmed real; the live serve-and-fetch half already worked earlier this session for `serveapp` (before this exact host-load spike) and rests on the exact same `tcp_serve_once`, so it is not a new, separately-broken path, just not re-confirmed live under tonight's specific conditions. Re-verify when the host isn't under unusual concurrent load.

## v11, a real desktop (this kernel gets a face), done (Sep 2026)
Everything v0-v10 built (graphics, font, mouse, HTML renderer, LLM chat, FAT) glued into one mouse-driven screen, no new subsystem needed.

## v12, voice mode: an LLM actually controlling this kernel, done (Sep 2026)
The v10 decision (voice stays a separate, gato-style host layer, no audio driver in the kernel) holds; this is that layer, one script, `tools/voice-control.sh`.

  Two real bugs, not glossed over. First: QEMU's monitor `sendkey` only accepts plain lowercase key names, an uppercase letter needs an explicit shift combo and most punctuation has no single-key name at all. The very first real test proved this the hard way: the LLM correctly answered "chat What is the capital of France?", but what actually landed in the kernel was "chat hat is the capital of rance", every capital and the question mark silently dropped. None of this kernel's commands need capitalization or punctuation to function, so the fix lowercases the LLM's command and strips anything that isn't a letter, digit, or space before typing it in, rather than mistype the answer into the kernel. Second, a plain prompt without examples mapped an open-ended question ("what's the capital of France") to the fallback "echo not supported" instead of the kernel's own `chat` command; fixed with a few-shot prompt showing that pattern explicitly, re-verified against four different spoken requests (a memory query, opening the GUI, a file listing, and an open-ended question) all mapping correctly afterward.

  Verified for real, twice over, end to end, not just the mapping step in isolation: since generating live human speech isn't something this session could do itself, used macOS's own `say` to synthesize real speech audio (a genuinely different, real signal, not a canned string), fed it through the real mic-recording-shaped pipeline. First run: synthesized "How much memory is free?" correctly became the kernel typing `mem` and printing the real, live frame count, confirmed by reading the kernel's actual VGA memory over the QEMU monitor, not by trusting the script's own printed claim. Second run, the more interesting one: synthesized "Ask the AI, what is the capital of France?" became the kernel typing `chat what is the capital of france`, which the kernel then ran for real, making its own separate network call to the same local Ollama server, and the real reply, "The capital of France is Paris.", appeared in the kernel's own VGA memory, an actual two-hop LLM chain (one model deciding what to run, a second one, inside the kernel, actually answering it), not a single call dressed up as two.

## v13, real file writing (this kernel can create a file, not just a directory), done (Sep 2026)
`mkdir` (v9) proved cluster allocation and directory-entry writing work; a real file needs the same machinery plus actual data clusters, no new concepts.

## v14, the GUI dock grows to fit the rest of the codebase's apps, done (Sep 2026)

## v24, real window chrome and cleaning up redundant branding (Sep 2026)
Direct follow-up requests after the mouse fix made the demo actually usable enough to notice these.
- [ ] [Joshua] Honest, not-yet-solved gap, raised multiple times in two different forms that turn out to be the same root cause: the landing page still letterboxes (black bars) on most real viewports, and the real local QEMU window can't be resized at all. Confirmed the second one directly, not assumed: setting the window's size via System Events while it was running was silently ignored, the window snapped right back to exactly 800x632, since this kernel's GUI is a fixed, hardcoded 800x600 with no mechanism for the guest to negotiate a different resolution with whatever's actually displaying it, browser canvas or native QEMU window alike. A real fix exists (`vbe_set_mode` already accepts any width/height; the missing piece is threading the actual display size into the kernel at boot, most likely via the multiboot command line, which isn't currently parsed for anything custom), but it's a real, multi-file change (embed.js computing and passing the size, kmain parsing it, `gui_run`'s hardcoded `window_open(800,600,32)` and every layout constant built around it going dynamic instead), not a CSS tweak or a QEMU flag, deliberately not rushed into the same pass as the mouse fix. Separately, real macOS native fullscreen (the green button, a Spaces transition) on the QEMU window is a known QEMU cocoa-backend limitation with custom Bochs VBE modes, not something this kernel can fix or something that needs a custom QEMU build; the practical workaround for now is not using it.

## v25, a real menu bar flicker fix, and a real, unresolved gap in test infrastructure (Sep 2026)
Direct bug report from actually running the OS locally for the first time this session, not the browser demo: "menu bar redraws and rerenders and flashes when we hover over any icon."
- [ ] [Fable] Re-diagnosed (Sep 2026), gap still real but the cause isn't what was written here before. The USB-tablet theory was wrong, disproved directly: `info mice` lists only the real PS/2 mouse, and a temporary serial log at `mouse_get_delta`'s real call site proved IRQ12 packets do arrive and do decode, one clean callback per `mouse_move`. What's actually broken: the decoded dx/dy don't correspond to what was sent, at all, not scaled, not offset, just different every run including movement reported on an axis given 0. That's QEMU's HMP `mouse_move` and/or this headless config's IRQ timing, not this kernel's PS2 driver or `guitest.sh`'s dock-geometry math, both of which are correct. See `guitest.sh`'s own header for the full trace. Not wired into any regression flow, not claimed to work.

## v28, real process exit and reap (task.c's half of the gap, done Sep 2026)
`task.c` shipped preemptive round-robin but never closed the gap it flagged honestly at the time (see the file's own old header comment): a task couldn't safely `return` from its entry function, so every demo task (`task_a`/`task_b`/`preempt_task_a`/`preempt_task_b`) had to park in `hlt` forever once done, permanently burning a slot out of `MAX_TASKS=6`. Running `tasktest` then `preempttest` used to leave 4 of 6 slots dead until reboot, exactly the constraint `reaptest`'s own predecessor tests warned about ("no free task slots, run fewer other task tests first").

## v29, a real VFS (done Sep 2026)
v4 shipped disk I/O straight against FAT16 because that was the only filesystem that existed, correctly deferring the VFS layer rather than build one for a single implementation to sit under. Real now: a second backend actually exists.

## v30, basic hacking tools (Kali/Mr. Robot flavored, done Sep 2026)
Direct request: a few small, real network-recon tools, not a security suite, exactly the "doesn't need to be anything crazy" scope asked for. Built on v7's existing real TCP stack, no new subsystem.

## v31 / 0.31.0, real per-task memory isolation (done Sep 2026)
Paging (v2) and ring 3 (v3) existed, but no task's page tables were actually distinct per task; ring-3 code proven to run at CPL 3 was a real, narrower claim than real process isolation. First real version under this project's new semver policy (see `VERSION`, `CLAUDE.md`): a genuinely new, backward-compatible capability, correctly a MINOR bump, no existing caller broke.

## v32 / 0.32.0, real signals: SIGKILL-equivalent (done Sep 2026)
Every task before this could only run to completion, fault, or be preempted-and-resumed at the exact instruction it left off; nothing could be told "stop" from outside itself short of tearing down the whole scheduler. This is what turns v28's reap from "cleans up after a task that already exited" into "can actually stop a runaway one." Real, backward-compatible new capability, correctly a MINOR bump.

## v33 / 0.33.0, a real block device abstraction (done Sep 2026)
`ata.c` was called directly by `fat.c`, the same "only one implementation exists, nothing to abstract yet" reasoning v4 correctly used for the VFS before v29. Real now: a second backend actually exists, same pattern as v29, applied one layer down.

## v34 / 0.34.0, real heap growth past the old 4MB wall (done Sep 2026)
`kheap.c` shipped with an honest, documented limit: it could only grow inside `paging.c`'s original identity-mapped first 4MB, refusing any frame at or past `0x400000` and just returning 0. That was correctly deferred, not an oversight, until something actually needed more, which v34's own `heapgrow` proof needed to actually happen (2048 real 4KB allocations, ~8MB, to cross the wall).

## v35 / 0.35.0, six more real apps ported, and a real dock overflow bug (done Sep 2026)
Direct request: scan the fleet and port every remaining app the existing single-static-file pipeline (`gen_app.sh`, v9) actually supports, not the whole 30+ app fleet (most are React/Vite or multi-file, a real separate project, see this file's own "Later product ideas" section). Screened every candidate's real extracted text (the exact `html_to_text` transform this kernel actually runs, not assumed from file size) rather than guessing from word count alone: `plan`, `lexly`, `conway` (ships as Toroid), `sparkjar`, `homeqi`, and `fieldbook` all have substantial real static copy; `epiphany`, `healstack`, `blockframe`, `numen`, `curvely`, `roost`, and `monocode` are JS-app shells whose real content only exists after client-side rendering this kernel can't run, correctly skipped rather than ported into a blank screen.

## Explicitly parked / non-goals
- SMP (multi-core), one CPU is plenty until everything above works
- A real filesystem journal / crash-consistency, FAT read support is enough for v4-v5
- Wi-Fi, wired NIC only, QEMU doesn't emulate Wi-Fi hardware anyway
- A real GUI toolkit (retained widgets, layout engine, a scene graph). v11's desktop is real and mouse-driven, but it's one hand-drawn screen with hardcoded hit-test rectangles, not a toolkit anything else could be built on top of. Build one if a second, different screen ever needs the same primitives enough times to justify it.
- gato (the macOS voice kiosk, `~/Documents/Code/gato`), different project, different repo, on purpose. Voice control of this kernel is a real future idea but nowhere near the front of the queue.

## Later product ideas (real, not scheduled, revisit once v8's browser actually renders to the framebuffer)
- Replace the landing page's recorded boot GIF with the real thing once there's something worth interacting with: an actual in-browser demo of this kernel, not a video of one.
- User accounts on the landing page, each with their own space.
- A downloadable, installable image so someone can put this on real hardware, not just QEMU, and actually boot into it (explicit north star, Joshua's own words). Real hardware brings its own driver-compatibility questions (this rtl8139 driver, the PCI enumeration, the ATA driver) that QEMU's emulated devices don't raise, worth its own pass when it's actually time, not assumed to just work.
- Real support for the entire codebase's fleet of apps, not just the handful hand-picked so far (explicit north star, Joshua's own words). v9/v11's `gen_app.sh` pipeline only handles the single-static-file case; most of the fleet is React/Vite (needs a bundler this kernel doesn't have) or split across multiple files `serve_app`'s one-shot single-connection server can't serve together. Getting from "5 apps" to "the whole codebase" is a real, larger project on its own, not a rename of the existing pipeline.
- A real text editor app for the GUI (Joshua's own request), with a font/typeface picker. `font.c` currently extracts exactly one embedded bitmap font (the real IBM CP437 dumped from VGA hardware); a picker needs at least a second real font to switch to, not a dropdown with one option, so this waits on that groundwork rather than shipping a fake selector.

## v48 / 0.48.0, boot screen dropped its wordmark, a real glass menu bar, and a native app icon (Sep 2026)
Three direct requests in one pass: "remove hello text on boot", "menu bar should be Liquid Glass semi transparent", "refresh macOS icon, make it look more native" (a real Dock screenshot supplied as reference).

  Boot splash now shows only the tree logo, `gui_draw_hello_script` (the hand-plotted 1984-Mac-style wordmark) left in place but uncalled, a real callback point if a wordmark comes back later rather than deleted code.

  Menu bar: no real alpha channel exists in this framebuffer (`gui_blend`'s own long-standing note), so "translucent" here is the same technique the dock's drop shadow already uses, a real solid, precomputed blend of white toward `gui_wallpaper_color(row)` per row rather than a flat 0x00FFFFFF fill, mostly white so text stays legible with just enough of the wallpaper bleeding through to read as glass. Not literal alpha compositing, an honest approximation within what this kernel's pixel format actually supports, same caveat every other "translucent"-looking element in this codebase already carries.

  Icon: `landing/icon.svg` already had the correct macOS squircle ratio (rx=44 of 200, ~22%) and a desert gradient, but read flat next to the real glossy Dock icons in the supplied reference screenshot. Added the two overlays every real Dock icon actually has and this one didn't: a soft white gloss ellipse across the top (linear gradient, fading to transparent by mid-icon) and a crisp 1.5px white inner rim light traced along the squircle's own edge, drawn outside the clip path so it isn't half-cut by the same rect it's tracing. `menubar/build-app-icon.sh` regenerates `icon.icns` from this file; re-run whenever `icon.svg` changes, not automated (`CLAUDE.md`'s no-daemon rule; the old AGENTS doc is gone).

  Verified: `make kernel.elf` clean (one pre-existing unused-function warning, not new), `check.sh` boots and reaches `gui_run`. The glass menu bar and gloss icon are both real, structurally verified (correct blend math, `iconutil` succeeded, `rsvg-convert` rasterized without error), not eyeballed on a real display yet, same honest gap as this project's other real-hardware/real-screen items, a human should look once.

## Icon sharpness, re-checked (Sep 2026)
Direct request: "keep the icon sharpening and polishing loop going." Read the actual current code before changing anything (checked, not assumed): dock icons already render through 6x supersampling (`ICON_SS_SCALE`), a real gloss band that follows the squircle's own curve (v43), a soft contact shadow, and a second dark offset pass under every glyph for lift, cached per (icon,size) so nothing re-renders on hover. This is the exact stack v41/v42/v43/v44.1 built and verified against real macro photos of the panel, not a guess. No further concrete sharpness bug found by reading the code; this headless setup can't screendump real pixels to check against the actual panel itself (`check.sh`'s own long-standing note: screendump renders solid black even when the kernel is running fine). Next real step here needs a fresh screenshot/photo of the current build to compare against, not another blind pass over already-verified code.

The live third-party integrations (Stripe, Quickbooks, Slack OAuth, etc.) are out of reach for what this kernel can actually do today, no TLS stack, no OAuth, `net.c`'s HTTP client is plain-text only, same honest limit `serveapp`/`web` already live within. What's real and buildable: the app roster itself.

## v50 / 0.50.0, DejaVu Sans instead of Mono for system UI text (Sep 2026)
Direct request: "system font needs Helvetica ... or even San Francisco." Real, findable bug, not a vague "make it nicer": v44's `gui_aa_char` hardcoded family index 2 (Mono) for every string in the OS via `font_set_aa`'s hook, so the menu bar clock, dock hover labels, and every app titlebar were rendering in a monospace/typewriter face, the exact "mono outside a char grid" mismatch this project's own house design rule warns against. Switched to family index 0 (Sans), the same DejaVu Sans table the Notes editor's own font picker already ships as an option, no new asset. Positioning unaffected: `font_draw_string`'s fixed 8px-logical advance per character places every glyph regardless of family, by design (see `font_set_aa`'s own note), so this was a pure typeface swap; the existing per-cell clamp (`x >= px + 16`) already clips a handful of wider proportional glyphs ('M','W') instead of colliding into the next character, same safety net Mono relied on.

Verified for real this time, not just "should look right": this session runs on the same Mac as its own attached display, so instead of QEMU's own broken headless `screendump` (real, long-standing, unrelated limitation, still true), launched the actual `-display cocoa` window this app's own launcher uses, resized it to something close to the kernel's real 960x540 logical canvas (QEMU cocoa windows DO resize now, unlike the stale note this roadmap carried from an earlier 800x600-hardcoded build), and took a real macOS `screencapture` of the live window, not a rendered mockup. The menu bar wordmark and clock read as genuinely proportional Sans now, confirmed by eye against the captured PNG.

Dock icon pass, re-benchmarked against a real Snow Leopard-era glossy dock (direct ask, real reference screenshot supplied): the tray, gloss band, and drop shadow already read close; the weather icon's sunburst reads a little muddy/low-contrast against the dark wallpaper and the terminal glyph is unclear at dock size, both real, specific, named targets for the next icon pass rather than another blind "sharpen everything."

## Accounts, passwords, real security (Sep 2026)
Direct request: solid permissions, security sandboxing, user accounts with passwords, "as secure as possible." Real, but sequenced, not something to fake a version of. This kernel has no actual privilege boundary to hang accounts on yet: `ring3_test` (v3) is a one-shot demo that manually drops to ring 3 and isn't a scheduled task, there's no `int 0x80` syscall gate (the honest gap already logged above), and every real task today shares the same address space and the same ring-0 access to everything, including the raw disk. Shipping a login prompt on top of that would store a real password and protect nothing: any code already running (any app, any shell command) could read it straight out of memory or off disk, no isolation to stop it. That's worse than no accounts at all, a false sense of security is a real vulnerability class, not a lesser version of a real one.
- [ ] [Joshua] Once that lands: a real threat model first, not a feature list. What is a Joshua Tree "user account" actually protecting against on a single-machine, single-visitor QEMU/browser demo, no network-facing multi-tenant use case exists today. Worth defining before writing a single line of auth code, the same way v31/v32 got the deeper Fable-level pass because a subtly wrong answer there looks fine and boots fine.

## v100 north star: talk to it with a real voice (Sep 2026)
Direct, explicit: "loop until version 100 or whatever, until we can basically talk to our OS with our microphone... hook up ElevenLabs eventually so we can get a visual or audible response, not just text." Recording the real shape of this rather than a vague aspiration:
- Real mic input needs the HDA/AC97 + USB audio driver chain already logged below, no shortcut around it.
- Speech-to-text and ElevenLabs (text-to-speech) are both real network API calls, same shape `chat`'s own Ollama call already proves out end to end (plain HTTP, real JSON), not a new kind of integration, just new endpoints once audio capture exists to feed them.
- gato (`~/Documents/Code/gato`) already solved "voice loop talking to an LLM" as a real, separate macOS app; the honest reason this kernel doesn't just reuse that shape directly is it needs its own real audio *driver* first (gato runs on macOS, with macOS's own audio stack underneath it for free). This is a hardware-then-software project in that order, not software-first.
- Realistic sequencing, most-blocking first: HDA/AC97 driver -> USB host controller + USB audio class (for a real Yeti) -> mic capture loop -> STT API call (reuse `chat`'s HTTP client) -> LLM call (already real) -> ElevenLabs TTS API call -> speaker output (needs the audio driver's output path too, not just input). Camera/video response is a separate, later branch off the same audio-driver prerequisite, not blocking voice.
Not started. This is the standing long-horizon target the loop keeps working toward, not a single item to check off.

## Later hardware ideas: mic + camera input (Sep 2026)
Direct request: real Yeti mic and camera input by v100, "unless that's a closed Apple API" plus a note that phone-as-webcam mirroring has existed for years. Real, worth recording accurately rather than guessing:
- Audio input needs a real driver this kernel doesn't have yet: QEMU emulates Intel HDA or AC97 (either is a real, documented, OSDev-wiki-covered device), nothing here reads from it today. A USB mic (a real Yeti) additionally needs USB host controller support (UHCI/EHCI/xHCI) and the USB audio class spec on top of that, a second real subsystem this kernel also doesn't have. Two real drivers, not one.
- A phone-as-camera/mic path (Continuity Camera on real macOS, or any phone-mirroring tool) is real and not Apple-API-gated at the OS level, but it only works because the *host* OS (macOS) exposes the mirrored phone as a normal AVFoundation camera/mic device to apps running on that same host. This kernel is the guest OS inside QEMU, a different machine as far as any driver is concerned; it would need the same real path any other guest OS does, QEMU passing a real (or virtual) camera/mic device through to the guest, which still bottoms out in this kernel needing its own real driver for whatever device QEMU presents (USB video class for a webcam, HDA/AC97 for audio), not a shortcut around the two items above.
- Realistic order once actually scheduled: HDA or AC97 audio first (single well-documented PCI device, closer to this kernel's existing driver shape, e.g. rtl8139.c/ata.c), USB host controller + USB audio/video classes after, camera input last (the largest, least-documented-for-a-hobby-OS piece). Not started, v100+ tier by the user's own framing, no code written against this yet.

## WiFi, real misconception corrected (Sep 2026)
Direct question: "we have wifi drivers in this Mac so [integrating wifi] should work, right?" No, and worth recording why precisely: this kernel's networking already works today through QEMU's emulated rtl8139 (a virtual *wired* NIC), and QEMU's `-net user` mode NATs that transparently through whatever real connection the host has, WiFi included, with zero WiFi-specific code anywhere in this kernel. The host Mac's real WiFi chip is never exposed to the guest at all, driver-level WiFi support only becomes meaningful once this kernel targets real hardware with a real 802.11 card (the existing real-hardware north star), and even then it's a genuinely large project on its own (a real chipset driver plus WPA supplicant), bigger in scope than the USB/audio items already logged above. Not started, not scoped for the emulator target at all since it's already unnecessary there.

Interactive verification (actually opening Reminders from the Apps folder, adding/toggling/deleting an item) needs a real human click: this session confirmed, for the first time directly, that simulated OS-level clicks (`osascript`/System Events `click at`) into the real `-display cocoa` QEMU window do not reliably reach this kernel's PS/2 input at all, a genuinely new finding, not the already-documented HMP `mouse_move` bug (that's QEMU's *monitor* protocol; this is a real, synthetic OS-level click into the actual window, a different path). Every earlier "an app opened" observation this session that looked like click confirmation turned out to be the *landing page's* browser-side idle tour coincidentally firing at the same moment, not this native app responding to a simulated click, caught by re-checking the timeline, not assumed. Real human verification still needed before calling the interactive path itself proven, same honest bar as `guitest.sh`'s own long-standing note on this exact class of problem.

## Graph as a native kernel app (Sep 2026)

## v54 / 0.54.0, Calendar (Sep 2026)
The grid alone, as scoped: `kernel/calendar.h`, one real month at a time, today as a filled clay disc, left/right (or up/down, or the Apps folder's own a/d) step a month with the year rolling over at either end, t jumps back to today, esc or a tap closes. Same shape as `reminders.h` in every way that matters (own header, `gui_draw_app_titlebar`, the `sleep_ticks(5)` + `mouse_click_edge_sync()` + `get_key_or_click()` loop), plus one small keyboard-driver addition: `KEY_LEFT`/`KEY_RIGHT` (0xE0 0x4B/0x4D, the same extended block up/down already decode), safe for every existing screen because each one already gates text input on `32 <= k < 127`. Date comes from the exact `cmos()` BCD registers `gui_draw_menubar` already trusts (7, 8), plus 9 (two-digit year) and 0x32 (century, with a 20xx fallback if a machine leaves it blank). Day-of-week is Zeller's congruence in its Gregorian integer form (`h = (q + 13(m+1)/5 + K + K/4 + J/4 + 5J) mod 7`, Jan/Feb as months 13/14 of the prior year, then shifted so 0 = Sunday to index the grid's own Sun..Sat columns directly), the textbook shape, not a homegrown table. New app slot 16 (`GUI_APP_COUNT` 18->19, `GUI_APPS_FOLDER`/`GUI_TRASH` shifted to 17/18), a new page-shaped glyph (`gui_icon_calendar`: solid header band with two binder rings punched through, a faint 3x2 day grid below with one square solid for today), a muted clay tile color (`0x00A0553F`, Mojave palette, not a saturated red). Apps folder only, not the pinned dock, same policy as Reminders. Per-day events (`EVENTS.TXT`) stay the explicit v2 they were scoped as.

Verified, the Fable-tag reason this was scoped separately: date math that "looks right, subtly isn't". Three real checks, not "it booted". First, by hand: 2026-09-14 (K=26, J=20, sum 177, 177 mod 7 = 2 = Monday, which is what today actually is), 2000-01-01 (m=13 of 1999, sum 259, 259 mod 7 = 0 = Saturday, correct), 1969-07-20 (sum 225, mod 7 = 1 = Sunday, correct), 2024-02-29 (m=14 of 2023, sum 201, mod 7 = 5 = Thursday, correct). Second, exhaustively on the host: new `tools/checks/check-calendar.sh` compiles the kernel's own `cal_dow`/`cal_days_in_month`/`cal_is_leap` with the host clang (pulled in under a `CALENDAR_MATH_ONLY` guard, nothing else from the kernel) and walks every day 1900-01-01 through 2099-12-31 against libc's `timegm(3)` `tm_wday` and month lengths: `73049 dates checked, 0 mismatches`. Third, inside the real booted kernel, end to end through the actual CMOS read and BCD decode: a temporary block in `kmain` (the same boot-time-marker trick `check.sh` itself relies on, removed before commit, `git diff` clean) compared `cal_dow(today)` against the RTC's own weekday register (cmos 6, 1 = Sunday) and wrote both plus the decoded date to RAM; `xp /4xw 0x9000` under headless QEMU read back `0xb007c0de 0xca1da7e5 0x07ea090e 0x00000201`: agree-marker set, decoded date 0x07EA-09-0E = 2026-09-14 (so the century/year registers decode right, not just the day/month the menu bar already used), RTC weekday 2 (Monday) matching Zeller's 1 (Monday, 0-based). `make kernel.elf` clean apart from the pre-existing `gui_draw_hello_script` unused warning, `check.sh` passes, `tools/checks/check-refs.sh` clean. Not verified this pass: the grid's actual rendered pixels (this was a headless sandbox with no attached display, so no `screencapture`), and `landing/v86/kernel.elf` deliberately not resynced for the same reason, that copy should be refreshed from a session that can look at the result first.

## v55 / 0.55.0, Calendar events (Sep 2026)
The real v2 v54 deferred: a persisted event per day, same file-per-app shape as `reminders.h`, now living directly inside `kernel/calendar.h` since this is a direct extension of the grid, not a new app. `EVENTS.TXT`, one line per date (`YYYY-MM-DD|text\n`), through the exact same `vfs_replace_file`/`vfs_read_file` pair Reminders and Notes already use, a fixed-size static array (`CAL_EVENTS_MAX` 64, `CAL_EVENT_TEXT_MAX` 40) with a `_loaded` guard so the file is only ever read once per boot, manual line-parsing (no `sscanf`), write-through on every mutation, no separate Save step. One event per date, not a list: a fresh add on a day that already has one overwrites its line in place (`cal_events_set`) rather than appending a second one for the same date, so both `EVENTS.TXT` and the dot/lookup logic stay single-entry-per-day; documenting that choice here since the task scope explicitly left it open.

`cal_date_str` builds the zero-padded `YYYY-MM-DD` string the file format and the grid's own lookup both key off of, same manual digit-building idiom as this file's own month title (`'0' + (vy / 1000) % 10`, ...) and `reminders.h`'s line-count formatting, no `sprintf`. It sits above the `CALENDAR_MATH_ONLY` guard next to `cal_dow`/`cal_days_in_month`, pure integer math like the rest of that section, specifically so `tools/checks/check-calendar.sh` can link straight to it.

The grid gained the one piece of real interaction it didn't have yet: a day cursor (`sel_d`, starting on today), moved with `[`/`]` rather than the arrow keys (already fully spent on month stepping, a real constraint the original task brief didn't account for, since v54 as actually shipped has no day-level selection at all to reuse, only month navigation). A light square renders behind the selected cell, independent of the "today" accent disc so both can show on the same day. Enter opens `cal_day_view`, a full-screen takeover-then-return view/add screen copied almost verbatim from `reminders_add_new`, pre-filling the edit line with that date's existing text if there is one. Changing month clamps `sel_d` into the new month's real day count instead of resetting to day 1 (so picking the 30th and stepping into a 28/29/30/31-day month still lands on a real day); `t` re-syncs `sel_d` to today the same way it already re-syncs `vy`/`vm`. Every day in the visible grid now also gets a small filled-dot indicator under its number when `cal_events_find` matches that day's `cal_date_str` against the loaded array, white when drawn over the "today" accent disc, clay-accent otherwise.

Verified for real, not "should work": `make kernel.elf` clean (only the pre-existing `gui_draw_hello_script` unused-function warning, not new); `./check.sh` -> `PASS: booted, reached gui_run`; `./tools/checks/check-refs.sh` -> `70 file-path references checked ... clean: every reference resolves somewhere in the real tree`. The actual date-matching logic, not just "it compiles": extended `tools/checks/check-calendar.sh` (moving `cal_date_str` above its `CALENDAR_MATH_ONLY` guard specifically to make this possible) to sweep `cal_date_str`'s output against the host's own `snprintf("%04d-%02d-%02d", ...)` for every one of the same 1900-2099 dates the existing Zeller sweep already walks, plus 9 explicit event-dot matching cases exercising the exact failure shapes that would matter (a short prefix like `"2026-09-1"` must not falsely match `"2026-09-14"`, day 4 must not light up day 14, zero-padded day 01 must not collide with day 10, a real leap day `2028-02-29` matches itself but not `2028-02-28`). Real output: `146098 dates checked, 0 mismatches` / `9 event-dot matching cases checked`. Honest gap, same category as v54's own: this was a headless sandbox with no attached display, so the actual rendered dot/selection-square pixels and the `[`/`]`/enter interaction haven't been confirmed against a real screenshot; `landing/v86/kernel.elf` deliberately left unsynced for the same reason. No changes to `kernel/kernel.c` at all this pass, the whole feature fit inside `calendar.h`, deliberately minimizing overlap with the concurrent syscall-ABI and notif-panel-font work also touching this repo.

## Notification panel: "bitmap font" was the cursor, root-caused and fixed (v56.1, Sep 2026)
Real, reproduced, screenshotted: the clock's notification dropdown (`gui_draw_notif_panel`) renders in the old blocky bitmap font, not the DejaVu Sans typeface v50 shipped everywhere else, confirmed live in the same screenshot as a correctly-antialiased menu bar sitting right above it. Structurally this goes through the same `font_draw_string` -> `font_draw_char` -> `aa_hook` path as everything else (confirmed by reading the code: no `window_push_target` anywhere in its own call chain, no conditional guard around `font_set_aa`'s one registration), so the cause isn't obvious from static reading alone. Tagged [Fable]: exactly the "looks like it should work, subtly doesn't" class this tag exists for, needs live debugging (a temporary serial print inside `gui_aa_char` to confirm whether it's even being called for this panel) rather than another guess.

Traced live, not guessed. A temporary serial print in `font_draw_char` logging (hook set, `window_scale`, `window_has_target`) for every glyph the panel draws came back `H2t` for all 356 of them: the AA hook IS called, at scale 2, with no offscreen target. The panel is drawn in DejaVu Sans, correctly, every time. So the font path was never the bug. What ruins it afterwards is the software cursor: `gui_cursor_save` read its backup through logical `window_get_pixel` (which samples only the top-left physical pixel of each 2x2 block) and `gui_cursor_restore` wrote it back through logical `window_pixel` (a full 2x2 block), so every cursor pass rewrote the antialiased text underneath as pixel-doubled text, which reads exactly like the old bitmap font. The menu bar and dock repaint on every hover and hid it; the notification panel is the one antialiased surface that is never repainted while open (the `cursor_only` tier deliberately touches nothing but the cursor), so the damage accumulated there alone and looked like a wrong font. Reproduced headlessly with no window: the panel forced open at GUI start, a scripted sweep of `gui_cursor_save`/`gui_draw_cursor`/`gui_cursor_restore` across it, then `pmemsave 0xfd000000 8294400 "/tmp/fb.raw"` through the QEMU monitor (the LFB address printed from `window_open_scaled`; the filename must be quoted or HMP parses `8294400 /tmp` as a division) converted BGRX to PNG with PIL. Before the fix the sweep left every crossed line blocky; after it, identical sweep, the text stays smooth. Fix: `cursor_backup` now holds `CURSOR_W*scale x CURSOR_H*scale` physical pixels via `window_get_pixel_phys`/`window_pixel_phys`, and the wind path's "backup must track the sway" refresh uses the same physical layout. `CURSOR_MAX_SCALE` (2) sizes the buffer; bump it with `gui_run`'s `window_open_scaled` factor. Verification was headless this pass (course-corrected mid-task: no stray cocoa windows on the real display while Joshua is watching it), plus one real `screencapture` taken before that correction confirming the untouched panel renders in DejaVu Sans. All debug prints removed, `git diff` is the fix only. Related, still open: the same logical save/restore pattern was hiding on every surface that does repaint (menu bar, dock band), so any future AA surface that isn't repainted per hover would have shown this too; now none will.

## Landing page cover/contain hybrid, real regression fixed same day (Sep 2026)
v52.2 (cover-fit, closing the demo's remaining letterbox) shipped, then a real photo from a narrow phone showed the menu bar's own clock/weather text cropped clean off the right edge, confirmed live, a real regression, worse than the letterbox it replaced. v52.9 fixed it same day: `resizeCanvas()` now only uses cover when it would still show >=75% of the source on its cropped axis, contain otherwise. Cover and contain are fundamentally at odds on an extreme aspect ratio (a 16:9 source in a much-taller-than-wide box has to either crop real content or leave real dead space), so this picks per-viewport rather than one global rule. Not yet re-verified live on a real narrow phone (only reasoned through the math and confirmed the desktop case still works); worth a real follow-up screenshot next time a phone is available.

## Weather-reactive wallpaper (Sep 2026)
Direct idea: make the animated wallpaper (v45's wind sway) dynamic based on the real weather data `menubar`'s own weather fetch already pulls in (temperature, condition text). Real and buildable later: e.g. wind intensity/sway amplitude scaled by a real wind-speed field if the weather API exposes one, or a simple condition match (rain/snow/clear) swapping a filter or particle effect over the existing photo. Not started, no scope decision made yet on which weather fields to react to or how (the current API call may not even fetch wind speed, needs checking before promising a specific effect).

Direct follow-up, a real, bigger idea than the sway/filter version above, worth recording as its own real scope, not conflated with it: use an LLM (a local model, e.g. Gemma, matching this repo's existing "talk to a local Ollama server" pattern `chat` already uses) to actually generate a new, animated version of the wallpaper photo itself, live, reflecting current real weather. Honest, real scope gap this needs before it's buildable: `wallpaper.h`'s `wallpaper_rgb` array is a compile-time constant, baked into the kernel binary at build time (see `gen_wallpaper.sh`); everything that reads it (`gui_wallpaper_row`/`gui_wallpaper_px`, the wind-sway sampler) assumes a fixed source image that never changes at runtime. A live AI-generated wallpaper needs real new plumbing this kernel doesn't have yet: a runtime HTTP call out to a local generation endpoint (the network/HTTP-client path already proven by `chat`), receiving real new pixel data over the wire (this kernel's plain-HTTP client, no image codec exists or is planned, so the far end would need to hand back raw RGB bytes in a format this kernel can use directly, not a JPEG/PNG it would need to decode), and a genuinely new *runtime-replaceable* wallpaper buffer instead of the current build-time-only one, kept in sync with the wind-sway/parallax code that already samples it. Not started, no scope decision made on the wire format or where the generation actually runs (this Mac's own Ollama, matching `chat`'s `10.0.2.2` host-gateway pattern, is the obvious first target). Deliberately still out of scope, a separate follow-up, not attempted alongside v60.

## v60 / 0.59.0, wind sway reacts to real weather (Sep 2026)
Ships the small half of the "Weather-reactive wallpaper" idea above, not the LLM-generated-wallpaper half (that stays logged separately, explicitly out of scope here: it needs a whole new runtime-replaceable wallpaper buffer this kernel doesn't have).

Checked honestly before building anything, per the roadmap note's own caveat: `weather_fetch`'s Open-Meteo call (`current=temperature_2m,weather_code`) does not request or parse any wind-speed field, confirmed by reading it, not assumed. So there is no real wind-speed number anywhere in this kernel to scale by. The real field that does exist and already reaches the desktop is `weather_code10`, the exact WMO code `weather_word()` already turns into the v56 dropdown's condition word (Clear/Cloudy/Fog/Rain/Snow/Showers/Storm). v60 drives sway off that: a new `wind_weather_pct` (default 100, the exact pre-v60 baseline amplitude) multiplies `gui_wind_shift`'s existing amplitude term, and `weather_fetch` sets it via a new `wind_pct_for_weather_code(code10/10)` right after a real fetch lands, using the same threshold boundaries as `weather_word()` so the sway always matches the word actually shown, not a second guess at the same code: Fog 40% (stillest), Clear 60%, Cloudy 100% (unchanged baseline), Snow 90%, Rain 130%, Showers 150%, Storm 200%. Those percentages are a plain judgment call, stated as one, not derived from any real wind measurement, since none exists in the fetched data; a genuinely more accurate version needs Open-Meteo's `wind_speed_10m` field added to the fetch, not attempted here (kept the fetch/parse surface untouched, only the field it feeds into is new). A NIC-less boot (v86, the browser demo) never calls `weather_fetch`, so `wind_weather_pct` stays at its 100 baseline and sways exactly as before v60.

Verified for real, not assumed: `make kernel.elf` clean (only the pre-existing `gui_draw_hello_script` unused-function warning), `./check.sh` -> `PASS: booted, reached gui_run`, `./tools/checks/check-refs.sh` -> clean. The actual amplitude change was proven, not just reasoned about: a temporary block in `kmain` (right after the existing `0x9000` boot marker, before `gui_run()`) pinned `wind_phase` to its max (256), then computed `gui_wind_shift(50)` three times under three fake `weather_code10` values (0 = Clear, 610 = Rain, 990 = Storm) exactly the way `weather_fetch` would set them, writing each result to fixed RAM (`0x9010`/`0x9014`/`0x9018`) and halting, no display needed. Booted under `-display none` with the monitor exposed on a local telnet port (avoided piping assembled input directly into QEMU's stdin, which this session's sandbox guard refused as an unverifiable construct) and read back with `xp /1xw`: Clear -> `0x700` (1792), Rain -> `0xf00` (3840), Storm -> `0x1800` (6144), matching the hand-computed values from the real formula exactly (base amplitude 12 at row 50, scaled 60/130/200% then by the pinned 256 phase). Three different, correctly-ordered real numbers, not "should work"; the debug block was then fully reverted (`git diff kernel/kernel.c` before commit touches only the four real additions: `wind_weather_pct`, its use in `gui_wind_shift`, `wind_pct_for_weather_code`, and the one call to it in `weather_fetch`).

Rebased onto `origin/main` (still at v59 / `3999b14` when this pass finished, no concurrent kernel.c landings in between) before tagging.

## Dock hover: real recording evidence, root cause still open (Sep 2026)
Direct bug report ("flashes/re-renders on hover, still present"), and this time actually reproduced with real evidence instead of another guess: `cliclick` (real HID-level CGEvent mouse movement, confirmed to actually reach this kernel's PS/2 driver where AppleScript's synthetic clicks earlier this session did not) driving the mouse across the dock in a windowed instance, captured with `screencapture -V` and split into frames via `ffmpeg`. Real finding: the Folder icon (slot 1) stays visibly magnified/lifted for roughly a full second (15+ consecutive frames at the capture's own frame rate) after the cursor has moved on to hover three, four, even five other icons, while the cursor sprite itself tracks correctly the whole time, confirmed side by side in the same frames. That's real evidence of state or redraw drift, not a testing artifact of a static screenshot.

Read, not guessed, before writing this note: `gui_dock_icon()`/`DOCK_ICON`/`gui_dock_hit_test`/`gui_slot_at` all recompute fresh from the same `dock_scale_pct` on every call (a macro-as-function, no stale cache), ruling out the theory that this session's own dock-scale-default change (10 -> 7) desynced the hit-test geometry from the drawn geometry. The animation-tick loop and the `dock_anim_changed` flag correctly appear in both the `dock_only` and full-redraw trigger conditions, so on paper a still-shrinking icon should keep forcing a repaint every ~3 ticks until settled, `cursor_only` explicitly requires `!dock_anim_changed` so it can't be silently winning over a real pending animation. Whatever's actually wrong isn't visible from static reading.

Honest open question this recording can't answer by itself: is this a real kernel bug (animation state genuinely not decrementing, or decrementing but not being presented), or an artifact of `cliclick`'s absolute-jump input pattern (QEMU's cocoa cursor handling might coalesce/clamp a big single delta differently than the many small deltas a real trackpad produces, the same general class of bug the web demo's own PS/2-packet-size note already describes for a different input path). Real next step: a temporary serial `klog`/print of `dock_hover_extra[1]` and `hover_slot` on every tick while reproducing this exact sequence, to see directly whether the kernel's own state is stuck or the presentation of already-correct state is what's stuck. Not fixed blind.

## Dock tray + icon shadow artifacts, icons "A-" (Sep 2026)
Direct feedback with two real screenshots of the native app at 1920x1080. Real, specific defects visible in them, not a vague "make it nicer": (1) the dock tray's rounded corners show mismatched pixels along the curve, the exact class `gui_rounded_rect_on_wallpaper`'s own comment already describes (a corner AA blended against one sampled wallpaper row while the real wallpaper varies row to row), plus a visible thin dark seam along the tray's top edge where it meets the wallpaper (likely the dock-band cache boundary or the shadow's first row); (2) every icon's soft contact shadow reads as a faint boxy/rectangular smudge under the icon rather than a clean soft ellipse, so `gui_draw_icon_shadow`'s falloff or its clipping against the tray isn't actually producing the round shape its comment claims; (3) icons overall rated "A-": the remaining gap is diagonal-edge aliasing on thin strokes (sun rays, the pen tip, the terminal glyph), confirmed earlier this session in a LANCZOS-interpolated zoom (so not a zoom artifact) even with 6x supersampling in place, meaning thin diagonals need either a wider stroke floor or a real coverage-based AA pass, not more supersampling. All three need real local screenshots to verify, so this is a local loop-pass item, NOT a remote-agent candidate.

## v61 / 0.59.1, dock top-edge seam + diagonal ray aliasing, both root-caused (Sep 2026)
The two real defects left open from the entry above, both root-caused this pass with the boot-time direct-call trick (draw the desktop, halt before the event loop, `pmemsave` the real physical framebuffer under `-display none`, convert BGRA->PNG, inspect actual pixels), not guessed at from reading the code alone.

**(1) The top-edge seam.** `gui_rounded_rect_on_wallpaper` only ever blended toward the real wallpaper pixel in its four rounded-corner regions (`cx >= 0 && cy >= 0`); every straight edge, including the whole top edge between the corners (most of the tray's own width), fell through to a plain `col = color`, a 100% solid fill with a raw 1-physical-pixel cut straight into whatever was behind it, zero pixels of blend. That was never visible as "aliasing" in the usual sense, since a perfectly horizontal boundary has nothing to alias, but it read as a real seam anyway because of where the tray sits: a real framebuffer dump of the row immediately above the tray's top edge, well away from any corner (x=700/900/1100/1300, physical y=905-917) read `(2,0,1)`, `(9,5,4)`, `(1,0,0)`, `(4,2,3)`, i.e. genuinely near-black (confirmed this is the wallpaper's own darkest gradient row generally, not a dock-local bug, by sampling the same row far from the dock at x=50/200/1700/1850 and finding the same near-black values there too), landing directly against the tray's light cream fill (`0xEFEBE4`) one pixel later with nothing in between. That single-row jump from near-black to cream is what read as a "dark seam." The dock-band-cache/shadow's-first-row guesses in the entry above were both wrong: the band cache (`gui_redraw_dock_band`) is never even in the call path for a freshly booted static desktop (`gui_draw_desktop` calls `gui_draw_dock` directly), and the hand-drawn "soft shadow beneath the tray" only ever paints rows *below* the tray's bottom edge (`sy = y0 + dock_h + row`), nowhere near the top. Fixed: a new `else if (py < band)` branch in the same loop, mirroring the corner branch's own style, blends the outermost `band` (3 physical px, the same width the corners already use) rows of the straight top edge toward the real per-pixel wallpaper color via the same `gui_wallpaper_sample` the corners call, straight-line distance from the true edge instead of the corner arc's radial one. Real before/after pixel proof, same four x columns, physical rows 918-921 (the tray's own first four rows): before, `918=(239,235,228)` immediately (full solid, no transition); after, `918=(3,1,2)` (still background), `919=(83,80,78)`, `920=(161,158,153)`, `921=(239,235,228)` (full tray), a smooth 3-step fade essentially identical in shape to the exact gradient v58's own corner fix already produced and shipped (`1 -> 81 -> 160 -> 239`).

**(2) Diagonal ray aliasing.** Every capsule stroke in this file except one already scales its radius with the icon's own size (`gui_icon_notes`'s `s/11`, `gui_icon_terminal`'s `s/16`, etc.); `gui_icon_weather`'s eight sun rays were the one exception, a flat literal `2` no matter how big `s` (the supersample buffer size) got. The four axis-aligned rays (N/S/E/W) still looked crisp at that width, since a horizontal or vertical stroke's perpendicular offset from its own centerline lands the same way on every row or column it crosses, nothing to alias. The four diagonal rays (NE/NW/SE/SW) didn't: rendered clean side-by-side comparisons at both real dock size and a deliberately large 160px direct render (to rule out "just too small to resolve" as the explanation) showed a genuine, visible sawtooth on the diagonal rays' edges that the cardinal rays never had, present even through this file's existing 6x supersample + 3x3 box-downsample pipeline, confirming the earlier LANCZOS-zoom finding rather than explaining it away as a zoom artifact. Root cause: at `2` (supersample-space) radius, the diagonal rays are under one physical pixel wide even after downsampling, thin enough that no reasonable amount of box-filtering produces a consistent partial-coverage average along a 45-degree line, the same class of problem as a sub-pixel hairline. Fixed: a real `ray_r = s / 34` (with the original `2` kept only as a floor for however small an icon this might ever be asked to draw), proportional like every other stroke in this file, scaled up enough that the ray's total width clears the sub-pixel threshold that was actually causing the aliasing. Real before/after proof: a 160px direct render of `gui_icon_weather` before the fix shows the described sawtooth clearly on all four diagonal rays under 4x LANCZOS zoom; the identical render after the fix shows clean, smooth-edged diagonal rays matching the cardinal rays' own crispness; the real dock-size (post-hover-magnify) render shows the same improvement, diagonal rays now clearly resolved instead of reading as faint slivers against the burgundy background.

Verified for real, not assumed: `make kernel.elf` clean (only the pre-existing `gui_draw_hello_script` unused-function warning), `./check.sh` -> `PASS: booted, reached gui_run`, `./tools/checks/check-refs.sh` -> clean (99 references). Headless the entire pass, no `-display cocoa` window opened at any point (the worktree-agent standing instruction), via the same boot-time direct-call trick this repo's other recent passes already used: a temporary `window_debug_fb_addr()` accessor plus a one-line marker write confirmed the LFB stayed at the same `0xfd000000` this session's earlier passes already found, then `pmemsave` under `-display none -vga std` pulled apart in Python (BGRA->PNG via PIL) for every real pixel value and crop quoted above. All debug instrumentation (the accessor, the marker write, a temporary large-icon diagnostic render + halt used only to get closer pixel evidence on the three named thin-stroke icons) fully reverted before commit; `git diff` against the shipped tree touches only the two real fixes in `kernel/kernel.c` (`gui_rounded_rect_on_wallpaper`'s new straight-edge branch, `gui_icon_weather`'s `ray_r`). Honest scope note: the pencil tip and terminal-glyph diagonals named in the original report were checked with the same real pixel evidence (both the normal dock-size render and a 160px direct render) and found to already be clean, no aliasing visible at either size; both already use a proportional radius (`s/11`, `s/16`) rather than a fixed literal, which is exactly the mechanism this pass root-caused as the actual cause on the sun icon, so no change was needed there. ## v58 / 0.57.1, dock icon shadow + tray corner AA fix (Sep 2026)
Ships the two root-caused, previously-diagnosed-but-unshipped defects from the entry this replaces. (1) Icon drop shadow read boxy because `gui_draw_icon_shadow` was the one dock element still drawn through the LOGICAL layer (`window_pixel`) while every icon tile beside it renders at physical res via the supersampled cache: at a ~38px icon, `ry = size/9` was 4 logical rows, so the whole ellipse was 9 rows of 2x2 blocks, and `sdx = dx*ry/rx` in integer math at ry=4 quantised the horizontal falloff into bands. Fixed: same math in physical units (`window_pixel_phys`, `rx`/`ry`/centre all multiplied by `window_scale()`), ry now 8 with real division headroom. (2) Tray corners showed mismatched pixels because `gui_rounded_rect_on_wallpaper` blended each corner's AA band against `gui_wallpaper_color(row)`, which samples the wallpaper at the CENTRE column of that row, while the corners sit at the tray's far left/right edges where the real wallpaper colour differs. Fixed: inside the corner branch only, now blends against the actual pixel behind it via `gui_wallpaper_sample(px, py, 0)` (the per-pixel physical sampler the wind code already uses; a forward declaration was added above `gui_rounded_rect_on_wallpaper` since the sampler is defined later in the file). (3) The thin dark seam along the tray's top edge is still undiagnosed, out of scope for this pass, tracked separately below.

Verified for real, not assumed: `make kernel.elf` clean (only the pre-existing `gui_draw_hello_script` unused-function warning), `./check.sh` -> `PASS: booted, reached gui_run`, `./tools/checks/check-refs.sh` -> clean. Mid-pass course correction, recorded honestly, same shape as v56's: started toward a live `-display cocoa` window + `screencapture` per the usual real-hardware standard, opened a couple of stray windows before the coordinator flagged that they were confusing Joshua watching the real, shared display (the one visible window is the main session's own `menubar/JoshuaTree.app` instance); those stray qemu processes were killed and this pass verified headless instead, via the same boot-time direct-call trick v54's Calendar pass and v56's weather-dropdown pass both already used: a temporary `window_debug_fb_addr()` accessor in `drivers/window.c` wrote the real physical framebuffer address (QEMU picked `0xfd000000`, confirmed via the monitor's `xp /1xw 0x9004`) into fixed RAM right after `window_open_scaled` in `gui_run`, then a `pmemsave` of the full 1920x1080x32bpp framebuffer under `-display none` was pulled apart in Python. Real pixel evidence, not reasoning-from-code: at the dock's top-left tray corner (around x=607-627, y=922-927), the AA band steps smoothly 1 -> 81 -> 160 -> 239 (background to full tray cream) with each blended pixel visually continuous with the true near-black local background sampled just outside the tray, no seam or mismatched "bubble" the way the old center-column sample produced; under an icon's bottom edge the shadow now shows a full ~9-level smooth radial gradient (138 -> 144 -> 153 -> 165 -> 180 -> 198 -> 219 -> 239) both horizontally and vertically, not the old 2-step boxy cutoff. Debug instrumentation (`window_debug_fb_addr`, the one-line marker write in `gui_run`) fully reverted before commit, `git diff` against the shipped tree touches only the two real fixes in `kernel/kernel.c`.

## Absolute pointer, "wherever you tap is where the mouse goes" instantly (Sep 2026)
Direct request after the mobile tap fix landed. Today every tap is relative: JS tracks a shadow cursor and sends paced PS/2 deltas, so the pointer visibly travels. The real fix is kernel-side: speak the VMware absolute-mouse protocol (the `0x5658` backdoor port, `VMMOUSE` commands; v86's own `mouse-absolute` bus path already implements the emulator side, read `landing/v86/libv86.js` for `vmware-absolute-mouse`/`push_absolute`), so the guest receives absolute coordinates and `embed.js`'s `moveCursorTo` homing/tracking machinery becomes unnecessary for taps. Verifiable headlessly via `mobiletest.mjs` (real Playwright iPhone emulation against the real page, already in the repo). QEMU's real cocoa display does not speak this protocol, so keep the PS/2 relative path as the fallback for the native app.
